I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both adjacent to the same node.
2. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating cliques (complete subgraphs) at each common neighbor.
3. Two nodes that share a common neighbor will now have a direct edge between them.

## Analysis of the Transformation

Let's think about what happens:
- In a tree, if two nodes u and v have a common neighbor w, then after adding the edge (u,v), they become directly connected.
- The resulting graph will have several connected components that are complete graphs (cliques) centered at each node that has degree ≥ 2.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, if we have a node w with neighbors u1, u2, ..., uk, then we add edges between every pair of these neighbors. This creates a complete subgraph (clique) among all neighbors of w.

## Special Cases

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** Tree with edges (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Node 1 has neighbors 2,3,4
- After adding edges: we add (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Now every pair of stations is directly connected (complete graph K4)
- Sum of distances = 0 (for same node) + 1 (for pairs) = 6 pairs × 1 = 6

**Example 2:** Tree with edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4)
- Node 2 has neighbors 1,3
- Node 3 has neighbors 2,4
- After adding edges: add (1,3) between neighbors of node 2, and add (2,4) between neighbors of node 3
- The resulting graph has edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,3), (2,4)
- Distance(1,4) = 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
- All other pairs have distance 1
- Sum = 6 pairs × distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7

## Algorithm

1. Build the tree adjacency list
2. For each node, identify its neighbors
3. For each node with degree ≥ 2, add edges between all pairs of its neighbors
4. Compute the sum of distances between all pairs

However, instead of explicitly building the new graph, we can work with the structure:

- All pairs of nodes that share a common neighbor will have distance 1 in the new graph
- Pairs that don't share a common neighbor will have distance 2 in the new graph (since they will be connected through some path)

Actually, let me think more systematically:

In the new graph, two nodes are connected by an edge if they were originally adjacent to the same node. This creates cliques at each node.

The key insight is that in the final graph:
- If two nodes share a common neighbor in the original tree, they have distance 1
- Otherwise, they have distance 2 (since they're in different cliques but connected through the graph structure)

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me reconsider the connectivity.

Actually, since every pair of nodes that have a common neighbor gets a direct edge, the graph becomes:
- For each node w, all neighbors of w form a clique
- The entire graph is connected because the original tree is connected

The distance between any two nodes u and v in the final graph:
- If u and v are in the same clique (they share a common neighbor), distance = 1
- If u and v are in different cliques that don't share a common neighbor, distance = 2

Is this always true? Let me verify with example 2:
- Nodes: 1 (neighboring node 2), 2 (neighboring nodes 1,3), 3 (neighboring nodes 2,4), 4 (neighboring node 3)
- Clique at node 2: nodes {1,3}
- Clique at node 3: nodes {2,4}
- Pair (1,4): 1 is in clique with 3, 4 is in clique with 3, so they both connect to 3, so (1,4) should have distance 1?

Wait, this doesn't match the example. The example says distance(1,4) = 2.

Let me re-read: "add new tunnels between any two stations u and v that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor"

So for nodes 1 and 4: do they share a common neighbor? 1 is adjacent to 2, 4 is adjacent to 3. 2 and 3 are not adjacent, so 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor. So no direct edge.

But in the new graph, will there be a path of length 2? Yes, 1-2-3-4 has length 3, but 1-3-4 (if 1-3 is added) gives length 2.

Oh! I see the issue. After adding edges, two nodes share a common neighbor when they were originally adjacent to the same node. So for node 2 (neighbors 1,3), we add edge (1,3). For node 3 (neighbors 2,4), we add edge (2,4).

So the new edges are (1,3) and (2,4).

The distance between 1 and 4: path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, both length 2.

So the final graph has edges:
- Original: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4)
- New: (1,3), (2,4)

So we can compute the distance between every pair.

## Final Approach

1. Build adjacency list
2. For each node, collect its neighbors
3. For each node with neighbors, add all pairs of neighbors to a set of new edges
4. Build a graph with original edges and new edges
5. For each pair of nodes, compute the shortest path distance
6. Sum all distances

But n can be up to 200,000, so we can't check all pairs directly.

Actually, we can use the observation that in the final graph, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2:
- If they have a common neighbor in the original tree, distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = 2

Is this always true?

Yes! Because in the original tree, any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor will still be connected, and since we're adding all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the only pairs with distance > 1 are those that don't have a common neighbor.

So distance(u,v) = 
- 1 if u and v have a common neighbor in the original tree
- 2 otherwise

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: All pairs have a common neighbor (all share neighbor 1), so all distances = 1. Sum = 6 pairs × 1 = 6. ✓

Example 2: 
- (1,2): neighbors of 1 is {2}, neighbors of 2 is {1,3}, common neighbor? 1 has neighbor 2, 2 has neighbors 1,3. 1 and 2 share no common neighbor? Wait, they are adjacent, so in the original tree, they are connected directly.

I think I need to reconsider. The edge (1,2) exists in the original tree, so they are already connected. In the new graph, we only add edges for pairs that weren't connected and share a common neighbor.

So distance in the new graph:
- For any edge in the original tree: distance = 1
- For any pair that don't share a common neighbor and don't have an existing edge: distance = 2

In example 2:
- Original edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4)
- New edges: (1,3) (since 1,3 both adjacent to 2), (2,4) (since 2,4 both adjacent to 3)
- All pairs:
  - (1,2): distance 1 (existing)
  - (1,3): distance 1 (added)
  - (1,4): distance 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
  - (2,3): distance 1 (existing)  
  - (2,4): distance 1 (added)
  - (3,4): distance 1 (existing)
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7. ✓

So the algorithm is:
1. Build original adjacency list
2. For each edge in the final graph, determine if it's original or added
3. For each pair of nodes (i,j), compute distance:
   - If (i,j) has a common neighbor in original tree AND they are not already connected originally, then distance = 1
   - Otherwise, distance = 1 (if original edge exists) or 2 (if no direct edge and no common neighbor)

Actually, simpler: 
- If there's a direct edge in the original tree, distance = 1
- Otherwise, if there's a common neighbor, distance = 1  
- Otherwise, distance = 2

Wait, but if they have a common neighbor, we add the edge, so distance becomes 1. If they don't have a common neighbor, and they weren't originally connected, distance becomes 2.

So distance(i,j) = 
- 1 if (i,j) has an existing edge (original) OR (i,j) share a common neighbor in original tree
- 2 otherwise

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [set() for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].add(v)
        adj[v].add(u)
    
    # Count total sum of distances
    total_dist = 0
    
    # For each pair of stations
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            # Check if already connected by original edge
            if j in adj[i]:
                total_dist += 1
            else:
                # Check if they share a common neighbor
                has_common_neighbor = False
                for k in adj[i]:
                    if k in adj[j]:
                        has_common_neighbor = True
                        break
                if has_common_neighbor:
                    total_dist += 1
                else:
                    total_dist += 2
    
    print(total_dist)
```

But this is O(n²) in the worst case, and n can be 200,000. This won't work.

We need to optimize.

## Optimized Approach

Let me think about this mathematically:

Total sum = sum over all pairs (i,j) of distance(i,j)

We can separate the pairs:
- Pairs with distance 1: original edges + new edges (pairs that share a common neighbor)
- Pairs with distance 2: pairs that don't have a common neighbor and don't have an original edge

Number of original edges = n - 1

Number of new edges = sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2) - (number of original edges among neighbors of w)

Actually, the new edges are exactly the pairs that share a common neighbor and don't have an original edge.

But in the final graph, distance is 1 for:
- All original edges
- All pairs that share a common neighbor (which adds new direct edges)

So the number of pairs with distance 1 = (n - 1) + sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2) - (number of original edges among neighbors of w)

Wait, no. Some pairs might already exist as original edges. 

Actually, for each node w, the pairs among its neighbors are:
- If there's an original edge between them, it's already counted
- Otherwise, we add a new edge

But in the tree, there are no edges between neighbors of w (unless w is a leaf or something).

In a tree, for any node w, its neighbors form an independent set - there are no edges between them in the original tree.

This is a key insight! In a tree, if u and v are both adjacent to w, then (u,v) is not an edge in the original tree.

Therefore, for each node w with degree d, we add C(d, 2) new edges.

Total new edges = sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

And total original edges = n - 1

So total number of pairs with distance 1 = (n - 1) + sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

All other pairs have distance 2.

Total pairs = C(n, 2) = n*(n-1)/2

Number of pairs with distance 2 = C(n, 2) - (n - 1) - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

Sum of all distances = (n - 1 + sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)) * 1 + (C(n, 2) - (n - 1) - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)) * 2
= (n - 1 + sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)) + 2 * (C(n, 2) - (n - 1) - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2))
= (n - 1 + sum) + 2 * (n*(n-1)/2 - (n - 1) - sum)
= (n - 1 + sum) + (n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum)
= n-1 + sum + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum
= n-1 + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - sum
= (n-1)*(1 + n - 2) - sum
= (n-1)*(n-1) - sum
= (n-1)² - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

Wait, let me recalculate step by step:

Sum = (n - 1 + sum) * 1 + (C(n,2) - (n - 1) - sum) * 2
= (n - 1 + sum) + 2*(n*(n-1)/2 - (n - 1) - sum)
= (n - 1 + sum) + (n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum)
= n - 1 + sum + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum
= n - 1 + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - sum
= (n - 1)(1 + n - 2) - sum
= (n - 1)(n - 1) - sum
= (n-1)² - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

So the answer is (n-1)² - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=4, tree has node 1 with degree 3 (neighbors 2,3,4), others have degree 1
- sum = C(3,2) = 3
- (4-1)² - 3 = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4, tree is a path 1-2-3-4
- node 1: deg=1, C(1,2)=0
- node 2: deg=2, C(2,2)=1  
- node 3: deg=2, C(2,2)=1
- node 4: deg=1, C(1,2)=0
- sum = 0+1+1+0 = 2
- (4-1)² - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

```python
n = int(input())

if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    deg = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    
    # Calculate sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    sum_c2 = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = deg[i]
        sum_c2 += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer is (n-1)^2 - sum_c2
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_c2
    print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In a tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if and only if they are at distance 2 in the original tree (through a common neighbor)
2. After adding edges between all pairs of nodes at distance 2, the resulting graph will have many connections
3. We need to analyze what happens to the distance between pairs of nodes

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about the transformation:

**Original tree structure:**
- For any node w, all its neighbors form a "cluster" where any two neighbors are now directly connected
- So for each node w, the subgraph induced by its neighbors becomes a complete subgraph (clique)

**Resulting graph structure:**
- The original tree plus complete subgraphs on each node's neighborhood
- This means that any two nodes that originally had distance 2 now have distance 1
- For nodes at distance > 2, the distance might decrease due to shortcuts

Actually, let me reconsider: when we connect all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're creating a complete graph on each neighborhood.

So for any node w with neighbors N(w), we add edges between every pair of nodes in N(w).

This means that in the new graph:
- Any two nodes that are adjacent in the original tree are still adjacent (distance 1)
- Any two nodes that were at distance 2 in the original tree now have distance 1
- For nodes at distance 3 or more, we need to analyze

Wait, let me think more systematically.

## Key Insight about Distance

After adding edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes u and v becomes at most 2, because:

1. If u and v were originally adjacent: distance 1
2. If u and v were at distance 2 through a common neighbor w: distance 1 (by new edge)
3. If u and v were at distance > 2: we might still have distance 2 or 1 depending on path

Actually, for any two nodes that are at distance 3 or more, they might still have distance 2 or 3.

But let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1:** Star graph (1 connected to 2,3,4)
- All pairs originally have distance 2 except adjacent pairs
- After adding edges: all pairs become distance 1
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** Path 1-2-3-4
- Original distances: (1,2):1, (1,3):2, (1,4):3, (2,3):1, (2,4):2, (3,4):1
- Sum originally: 1+2+3+1+2+1 = 10
- After adding edges between nodes sharing common neighbors:
  - Nodes sharing neighbor 2: 1 and 3 (already connected)
  - Nodes sharing neighbor 3: 2 and 4 (already connected)
  - So we add edge between 1 and 3, and between 2 and 4
- New distances:
  - (1,2):1, (1,3):1 (direct), (1,4):2 (1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
  - (2,3):1, (2,4):1 (direct), (3,4):1
- Sum: 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## Algorithm

The key insight: after the operations, the graph becomes such that:
- Any two nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree have distance 1 if they're at distance 2, or 1 for adjacent
- Any two nodes at distance 3 or more might still have distance 2

Actually, let me reconsider: for any two nodes u and v that are not adjacent in the original tree, their distance is min(original_distance, 2).

Why? Because if they are at distance 2, we add a direct edge. If they are at distance > 2, we don't have a direct edge, but we might have shorter paths.

Actually, the maximum distance in the new graph is 2. This is because:
- Any two nodes that have a common neighbor now have a direct edge
- Any two nodes that don't have a common neighbor might still be connected through paths of length 2

So in the new graph, the distance between any two nodes u and v is:
- 1 if u and v are adjacent in original graph OR if they have a common neighbor in original graph
- 2 if they don't have a common neighbor and aren't adjacent

But wait - in a tree, if u and v don't have a common neighbor, they might still be connected through other paths, but we only add edges for common neighbors.

Actually, in a tree, two nodes have a common neighbor if and only if they are at distance 2 in the original tree.

So the distance in the new graph is:
- 1 if original distance ≤ 2
- 2 if original distance ≥ 3

Let me verify with example 2:
- Original distances: (1,2):1, (1,3):2, (1,4):3, (2,3):1, (2,4):2, (3,4):1
- New distances: (1,2):1, (1,3):1, (1,4):2, (2,3):1, (2,4):1, (3,4):1
- Sum: 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

Yes! This matches.

## Solution Approach

1. Build the tree from input
2. Precompute distances between all pairs of nodes in the original tree
3. For each pair (i,j), if original distance ≤ 2, distance in new graph = 1
4. If original distance ≥ 3, distance in new graph = 2
5. Sum all these distances

But n can be up to 200,000, so we can't compute all pairwise distances.

## Optimized Approach

We want to compute the sum of distances in the new graph.

Let me think differently: sum of distances = sum over all pairs (i,j) of distance(i,j) in new graph.

We can separate this sum:
- For pairs with original distance 1: contribute 1 each
- For pairs with original distance 2: contribute 1 each  
- For pairs with original distance ≥ 3: contribute 2 each

So total sum = (number of pairs with dist=1) + (number of pairs with dist=2) + 2*(number of pairs with dist≥3)

But we also know that in a tree, the total number of pairs is n*(n-1)/2.

And the total sum of original distances is something we can compute, but we don't need it directly.

Actually, we can compute:
- Count of pairs with distance 1: this is (n-1) because tree has n-1 edges
- Count of pairs with distance 2: we need to compute this
- Count of pairs with distance ≥3: total pairs - (pairs with dist=1) - (pairs with dist=2)

Wait, no: the pairs with distance ≤2 already give distance 1 in new graph, pairs with distance ≥3 give distance 2.

But actually: every pair at distance 1 or 2 in original gets distance 1 in new graph, every pair at distance ≥3 gets distance 2 in new graph.

So total sum = (number of pairs at distance 1 or 2) * 1 + (number of pairs at distance ≥3) * 2

= (total pairs) + (number of pairs at distance ≥3)

Because: pairs at dist 1 or 2: +1, pairs at dist ≥3: +2, so total = total_pairs + (number at dist ≥3)

Now we need to compute the number of pairs at distance ≥3.

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
Number of pairs at distance ≥3 = total_pairs - (pairs at distance 1) - (pairs at distance 2)

So final sum = total_pairs + (total_pairs - (pairs at dist 1) - (pairs at dist 2))
= 2 * total_pairs - (pairs at dist 1) - (pairs at dist 2)

We know pairs at dist 1 = n - 1

We need to compute pairs at dist 2.

## Computing Pairs at Distance 2

For each node w, let deg(w) be its degree. 
The number of pairs at distance 2 that go through w is C(deg(w), 2) = deg(w)*(deg(w)-1)/2.

But this counts every pair (u,v) where u and v are neighbors of w. 
Since each pair at distance 2 is uniquely determined by its common neighbor w (in a tree), we can sum over all nodes.

So pairs at distance 2 = sum over all w of C(deg(w), 2)

Therefore:
final_sum = 2 * (n*(n-1)/2) - (n-1) - sum of C(deg(w), 2) over all w
= n*(n-1) - (n-1) - sum of C(deg(w), 2)
= (n-1)*(n-1) - sum of C(deg(w), 2)
= (n-1)^2 - sum of C(deg(w), 2)

Wait, let me verify with examples:

**Example 1:** n=4, star graph
- deg(1)=3, deg(2)=1, deg(3)=1, deg(4)=1
- sum of C(deg(w), 2) = C(3,2) + 3*C(1,2) = 3 + 0 = 3
- final_sum = (4-1)^2 - 3 = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- deg(1)=1, deg(2)=2, deg(3)=2, deg(4)=1
- sum of C(deg(w), 2) = C(1,2) + C(2,2) + C(2,2) + C(1,2) = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- final_sum = (4-1)^2 - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list and compute degrees
    deg = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    
    # Compute sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes
    sum_c2 = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = deg[i]
        sum_c2 += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Final answer: (n-1)^2 - sum of C(deg(w), 2)
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_c2
    print(result)
```
